**In 2006 somebody asked for the number 52 and was told no.** The designation X-52A was requested for a programme to test active aeroelastic wing technology, and it was disapproved because of possible confusion with the B-52 series, and the designation X-53A was allocated to the programme instead [[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]].

This is the fifty-third article in the [X-Planes series][related_post_a297_framing], following the [X-1][related_post_a298_bell_x1], the [X-2][related_post_a299_bell_x2], the [X-50][related_post_a347_boeing_x50], and the [X-51][related_post_a348_boeing_x51].

**Every other article in this series is about an aeroplane, or about a number that never became one.** This one is about a number that was refused, which is a different thing again. The [X-39][related_post_a336_x39_reserved_never_assigned] was reserved and then lost because nobody wrote the letter that would have assigned it [[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]]. The X-52 was asked for in writing, considered, and declined. **The paperwork was done correctly and the answer was no.**

**The reason given is the whole of the article.** Not that the aircraft was unworthy, not that the programme was cancelled, and not that the number had been used. The number was refused because of how it would sound next to a number that already existed.

## What the Record Says Happened

**The request was for X-52A and the answer was X-53A.** The specialist registry that maintains the record of United States military aerospace vehicle designations states the case in two sentences and they are the only two sentences of primary description that exist in public [[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]]. The designation X-52A was requested in 2006 for a programme to test active aeroelastic wing technology. It was disapproved because of possible confusion with the B-52 series, and X-53A was allocated instead.

**The programme was the Active Aeroelastic Wing.** A modified F/A-18A, flown at the space agency's flight research centre at Edwards in cooperation with the Air Force Research Laboratory and Boeing Phantom Works, to find out whether a wing can be twisted deliberately to roll an aeroplane rather than being stiffened until it stops twisting [[Active Aeroelastic Wing Flight Research, NASA Facts FS-2005-03-061 DFRC][ref_aaw_factsheet]] [[Air Force Research Laboratory][ref_afrl]]. **It had already flown before it had a number.** The programme began in 1996, the aircraft first flew in modified form in November 2002, and the flight research concluded in March 2005 [[Flight test of the F/A-18 active aeroelastic wing airplane][research_aaw_flight_test]].

**The number arrived over a year after the flying stopped.** The aircraft was designated X-53 on 16 August 2006, by memorandum of the Air Force Deputy Chief of Staff for Strategic Plans and Programs [[Boeing X-53 Active Aeroelastic Wing][ref_x53_wikipedia]] [[F/A-18 Active Aeroelastic Wing, NASA][ref_nasa_x53]]. **What the aeroplane did with the number is the next article's subject and this one leaves it there.**

### The Office That Said No

**The decision was made by one office and the instruction names it.** A request for a designation goes from the programme office to the Department of Defence Control Point, which is the Air Force Asset Identification Branch at Wright-Patterson, and that office checks the proposal against the regulations and forms its own recommendation. The recommendation and the original request then both go to the Directorate of Programs, Program Integration Division at the Pentagon, where the decision is made [[Allocation of Official Aerospace Vehicle MDS Designations][ref_mds_allocation]].

**That office changed its own designator in the year it refused this one.** Its symbol was HQ USAF/XPPE and became AF/A8PE with effect from 1 February 2006 [[Allocation of Official Aerospace Vehicle MDS Designations][ref_mds_allocation]]. **The office that refused a number for resembling another number spent that same year renaming itself**, and nothing in the record suggests anybody noticed.

**Refusal is the exception and not the rule.** The registry's account of the process records that in most cases the originally requested designation is approved even when the coordinating office recommends a different one, and gives the F-35 and the KC-767A as the examples [[Allocation of Official Aerospace Vehicle MDS Designations][ref_mds_allocation]]. **A request that is refused outright is therefore unusual**, and this one was refused on a ground the instruction does not mention.

## The Rule That Refused It Is Not in the Instruction

**The governing document is a joint instruction of the Air Force, the Army and the Navy, and the issue in force in 2006 was dated 14 April 2005** [[Air Force Joint Instruction 16-401, 14 April 2005][ref_afi_16_401_2005]]. It is not long. It says what a designation is made of, who may ask for one, and who decides. **It was read for this article, in full, and the reading is the article's central evidence.**

**It requires the next number and provides no way of skipping one.** Section C, paragraph 5 directs that the coordinating office will assign and reserve the next available consecutive design number within each basic mission for new vehicles. There is no qualification, no exception and no discretion attached to it. Formally, if $A_m$ is the set of design numbers already allocated within basic mission $m$, the instruction defines what is to be assigned as

$$n^{\star}(m) \;=\; \min\bigl\{\,k \in \mathbb{N} \;:\; k \notin A_m \,\bigr\}$$

and in 2006 the X series held 1 through 51, so $n^{\star}(X) = 52$. **The number that was refused is the number the instruction names.**

**The word skip does not appear in the 2005 issue at all.** Neither does discretion, nor any authority to decline a design number, nor any criterion by which a proposed number might be judged unsuitable. **The document that governed the decision contains no provision under which the decision could be made.**

**Nor did the issue before it.** The 1994 issue of the same joint instruction was read as well, and it says the same things in the same order. The coordinating office will assign the next available consecutive design number within each basic mission for new vehicles. Design numbers run consecutively from 1 to 999. Requested popular names are checked for duplication against the master list, the Judge Advocate General conducts a trademark search of them, and final coordination comes from the Assistant Secretary of Defence for Public Affairs. The only mention of confusion attached to a designator is the prohibition of I and O as series symbols [[Air Force Joint Instruction 16-401, 1994][ref_afi_16_401]] [[United States Department of Defense Aerospace Vehicle Designation][ref_dod_designation]].

**So the instruction carried no authority to skip a design number for at least twenty-six years**, across two issues, while the practice of skipping them ran continuously through the same period and produced C-16, C-42, C-43, C-44, P-6, F-19 and every design number 13 in the system.

### The Instruction Does Screen for Confusion, and It Screens the Other Half of the Name

**This is not a document that forgot about confusion.** It has an entire apparatus for it, and the apparatus is pointed somewhere else. Section E governs the popular name, meaning the word Stratofortress rather than the string B-52. A requested popular name is checked for duplication against the master list of popular names. It is reviewed by public affairs at command level and then at service level. **The Air Force Judge Advocate General conducts a trademark search of the proposed name.** It is coordinated among the services, the Secretary and the Chief of Staff both coordinate on it, and the Assistant Secretary of Defence for Public Affairs concurs or does not [[Air Force Joint Instruction 16-401, 14 April 2005][ref_afi_16_401_2005]].

**A design number goes through none of that.** It is issued in sequence by a logistics office.

**And the instruction says plainly which half is the official one.** Section F states that the designation is the official identification, that it represents a specific category of vehicles for operations, support and documentation, and that the popular name characterises the mission and aids communications and media references [[Air Force Joint Instruction 16-401, 14 April 2005][ref_afi_16_401_2005]]. **So the confusability controls, the duplication check, the legal search and the four levels of review, sit on the informal half of the identifier, and the half the instruction calls official is issued by a counter.**

### The Only Written Anti-Confusion Rule for the Designator Concerns Two Letters

**There is exactly one.** The series symbol, the letter that follows the design number and identifies the production model, runs consecutively from A, and the instruction prohibits the letters I and O for that symbol in order to avoid confusion [[Air Force Joint Instruction 16-401, 14 April 2005][ref_afi_16_401_2005]]. **The confusion in question is with the digits 1 and 0**, which is a claim about how a character looks on a page or a panel.

The consequence is a smaller alphabet,

$$|S| \;=\; 26 - 2 \;=\; 24,$$

so a design number supports twenty-four production models before the instruction requires the design number itself to advance to the next unused value.

**That rule is the whole of the system's written thinking about a confusable designator, and it is about typography.** It does not consider what happens when the number is spoken. It does not consider another mission series at all.

### The Authority Was Written Down Fourteen Years Later

**The instruction was reissued on 3 November 2020**, jointly as a Department of the Air Force Instruction, an Army Regulation and a Naval Air Systems Command Instruction [[Department of the Air Force Instruction 16-401, 3 November 2020][ref_dafi_16_401_2020]]. It carries a clause that the 2005 issue does not.

**Attachment 2, paragraph A2.1.6.1.2 reads that AF/A8PE reserves the authority to skip design number at discretion.**

**That is one sentence, it names the office that refused the X-52A, and it supplies no criterion whatsoever.** It does not say to avoid confusion. It does not say when, or on what evidence, or subject to what review. **It converts a thing that office had been doing into a thing that office is entitled to do**, and it does so without describing the thing.

**Every other provision quoted above survives into the 2020 issue unchanged in substance.** Design numbers still run consecutively. The series symbol still forbids I and O to avoid confusion with the design number. The popular name still goes to a trademark search and still gets its four levels of review [[Department of the Air Force Instruction 16-401, 3 November 2020][ref_dafi_16_401_2020]]. **The discretion to skip a number is the addition, and it is the only one that matters to this article.**

## The Same Office Had Manufactured the Same Collision Four Years Earlier

**In 2001 the Joint Strike Fighter needed a designation and the sequence said F-24.** The nomenclature office recommended exactly that, on the stated ground that design numbers are to be assigned consecutively, which is the same provision the X-52A refusal set aside [[Non-Standard DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_nonstandard_mds]].

**It was overruled.** The number that was approved on 5 June 2002, by HQ USAF/XPPE, was 35 [[Non-Standard DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_nonstandard_mds]] [[Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II][ref_f35]]. It came from a press conference, at which the Under Secretary and the programme manager announced the production aircraft by taking the demonstrator's designation and replacing the X with an F. **No designation had been reserved or approved for the production aircraft at that point at all.**

**So the F-35 exists because somebody deliberately made a production fighter share a number with an experimental aeroplane across a series boundary** [[Lockheed Martin X-35][ref_x35]].

**Four years later the same office, under a symbol it had changed in the interim, refused a number because it would have shared a number with a bomber across a series boundary.**

**The structure of the two cases is identical.** In each, two designations differ only in the mission symbol and agree in the design number. In 2002 that identity was treated as desirable continuity worth breaking the sequence rule to obtain. In 2006 it was treated as a hazard worth breaking the sequence rule to avoid. **The instruction in force was the same document in both years and it authorised neither.**

## What the Anomaly Reveals About the Designation System

### The Refusal Belongs to a Documented Family

**The X-52 is not an isolated decision, and the registry that records it records the others** [[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]].

**C-16 was skipped, and the reported reason names the mechanism precisely.** The number was on reservation for the aircraft that became the C-17, and the nomenclature office recommended C-16A on the ground that 16 was the next available number and that skipping it conflicted with the regulation then in force. One day later that letter was withdrawn and replaced by one concurring in C-17, and C-16 was to be marked in the master list as not used. No reason was recorded. **It is reported that the number was skipped because of concerns over confusing the plane with the F-16 during the stress of high combat radio traffic** [[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]] [[Boeing C-17 Globemaster III][ref_c17]] [[General Dynamics F-16 Fighting Falcon][ref_f16]].

**That sentence is the clearest statement in the whole record of what these refusals are actually about.** Not the register, not the paperwork, and not the eye. A voice, a radio, and a crew under load.

**C-43 and C-44 were skipped to avoid confusion with the existing T-43 and T-44**, and the registry reports unofficial information from the department that an informal policy is in effect to avoid duplication of well-known numbers [[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]]. **An informal policy is precisely a rule that is not written down**, which is the same finding as the X-52 case reached from the other side.

**P-6 was skipped for the Navy in 1962**, on the nomenclature forms themselves, most probably to avoid future confusion with the P6M. **T-50A is reserved and unassigned** because a foreign trainer is marketed under that number and assigning it to a different model was regarded as too confusing. **C-42 was skipped** over concern about legal exposure to a widely sold light aircraft marketed as the C42 [[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]].

**XV-14 was allocated and then withdrawn, and the withdrawal letter is quoted.** In May 1973 the Army and the space agency asked for a designation for a tilt-rotor demonstrator and XV-14 was allocated on 30 May. On 7 August a change was requested to avoid confusion with the Bell X-14B, and the request argued the point at length. Both aircraft were built by companies known shortly as Bell. Both would be flying at the same research centre. It warned of serious errors if the service and maintenance records of the two were mixed up, and of unnecessary resultant confusion to the aeronautical research and development community in general. The redesignation was approved on 22 August and the aircraft became the XV-15 [[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]] [[Bell XV-15][ref_xv15]].

**And F-19 was skipped under a stated reason that the registry says was not the real one.** The official explanation was to avoid confusion with the MiG-19. What actually happened is that the manufacturer asked to skip it because it wanted an even number to distinguish its export fighter from Soviet competitors that all used odd ones, and the approving letter repeated the confusion story anyway [[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]] [[Northrop F-20 Tigershark][ref_f20]]. **Confusion is available as a reason whether or not it is the reason**, which is worth holding in mind when reading a two-sentence entry about the X-52.

**A number is also skipped for reasons that have nothing to do with confusion.** F-13 was rejected by the manufacturer or the Navy for superstitious reasons, and since the late 1960s it has been an unwritten rule that a design number 13 is always skipped, which has cost A-13, C-13, G-13, Q-13 and V-13 as well [[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]]. **A system that skips every thirteenth number by unwritten custom is not a system that will find skipping the fifty-second difficult.**

### Every One of These Collisions Crosses a Series Boundary

**Look at what is actually colliding in each case.** X-52 with B-52. C-16 with F-16. C-43 and C-44 with T-43 and T-44. V-14 with X-14. P-6 with P6M. **In every instance the two designations differ in the mission symbol and agree in the design number.**

**A designation is formally a pair.** Writing $M$ for the set of basic mission symbols and $A_m$ for the numbers issued within mission $m$, an assigned designation is an element of

$$\mathrm{MDS} \;=\; \bigl\{ (m, n) \;:\; m \in M,\; n \in A_m \bigr\},$$

and within that space the pairs are unique by construction, because each mission counts its own sequence. **X-52A and B-52H are distinct elements and no rule of the instruction is broken by having both.**

**The refusals are evidence that somebody did not believe the first coordinate survives the journey.** If the channel a designation actually travels down preserves only the number, the effective identifier is the projection

$$\sigma(m, n) \;=\; n,$$

and under $\sigma$ the two designations are the same object. **The C-16 reason names that channel explicitly, and it is a radio.**

**The two spaces are of very different sizes and that is the entire problem.** The register holds

$$\sum_{m \in M} \bigl| A_m \bigr|$$

distinct designations, while a listener who hears only the number can distinguish at most

$$\Bigl| \bigcup_{m \in M} A_m \Bigr| \;=\; \max_{m \in M}\, \max A_m$$

of them. **The first grows with the number of mission series and the second does not.** Adding a mission symbol adds capacity to the register and adds none at all to the radio.

**Collisions are therefore not accidents in this system. They are its arithmetic.** Writing $o_k$ for the number of mission series that have issued the design number $k$,

$$o_k \;=\; \bigl| \{\, m \in M \;:\; k \in A_m \,\} \bigr|,$$

the count of colliding pairs across the whole register is

$$C \;=\; \sum_{k \geq 1} \binom{o_k}{2},$$

which is zero only if no number is ever issued twice across all series. **Every low design number in this system has been issued in several series**, because every series starts at one and counts up. There is an X-4 and an F-4. There is an X-5 and an F-5. There is an X-15 and an F-15, an X-16 and an F-16, an X-22 and an F-22, an X-35 and an F-35 [[List of X-planes][ref_list_of_x_planes]] [[1962 United States Tri-Service Aircraft Designation System][ref_tri_service_1962]].

**If the mission letter is lost, the chance of attributing a spoken number to the right series is at best**

$$P(\text{correct} \mid k) \;=\; \frac{1}{o_k}$$

under a uniform prior over the series holding that number. **This is an idealisation and the article says so**, since a listener has context, expectation and an aerodrome full of clues. It is stated because it shows the shape of the thing. **The quantity the system controls is $o_k$, and the system increases it every time a new series is opened.**

### The System Declined to Fix the Confusion It Actually Had

**Two designations were requested in 1953 for the express purpose of ending a confusion that was already happening, and both requests were refused.**

The Wright Air Development Center asked to redesignate a drone converted from the B-17 as Q-7, and a drone converted from the F-80 as Q-8. **The stated reason was problems and delays in the production and support of the drones, because they were too easily confused with unmodified aircraft of the type they were built from.** That is not a hypothetical about a radio. It is a report of a supply system misdelivering. **The Air Research and Development Command did not concur with the reasoning and turned both requests down in March 1954** [[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]].

**Set the two decisions beside each other.** In 1954 an office declined to change a designation although confusion was occurring and was costing time and parts. In 2006 an office declined to issue a designation because confusion might occur. **The same system, on the same question, refused to act on evidence and acted on possibility.**

**That asymmetry is the sharpest thing the X-52 case reveals**, and it is visible only because the registry preserved the failures as carefully as the successes.

### A Number Is Occupied for as Long as the Aeroplane Lives

**A design number becomes unavailable when it is allocated and stays unavailable until the last aircraft carrying it retires**, since the instruction directs that a retired designation is not used again for another vehicle [[Air Force Joint Instruction 16-401, 14 April 2005][ref_afi_16_401_2005]]. The duration is

$$T_{\text{occupied}} \;=\; t_{\text{retire}} \;-\; t_{\text{allocate}},$$

and for most aeroplanes this is a few decades.

**The B-52 is the extreme value of that quantity and it is not close.** It entered service in 1955 and is planned to fly into the 2050s after re-engining, which is a working life approaching a century and one no other combat aircraft has approached [[Boeing B-52 Stratofortress][ref_b52]]. Taking 1955 and 2050 gives

$$T_{\text{occupied}} \;\geq\; 2050 - 1955 \;=\; 95 \ \text{years},$$

and at the moment of the refusal in 2006 the aeroplane had already held the numeral for fifty-one of them.

**So the request in 2006 was not unlucky in some general way.** It asked for the one number in the low range of the sequence that is attached to the longest-serving combat aircraft in the history of the thing. **Had the X series reached 52 in 1948 the answer would have been yes, and had it reached 52 in 2048 the answer would probably still be no.**

**The aeroplane whose number blocked the X-52 appears in twelve of the fifty-two other articles this series has drafted, which is 23.1 percent of them, across 26 mentions.** It carried the X-15, the X-24, the X-38, the X-43 and the X-51 aloft. **The article immediately before this one is about an aeroplane a B-52 dropped**, and the number immediately after that aeroplane's was refused because of the aeroplane that dropped it.

### What Was Lost Was Nothing, and That Is the Point

**Compare the two anomalies this series has now examined in the reduced order.**

**The [X-39][related_post_a336_x39_reserved_never_assigned] produced a permanent hole.** The number was reserved and the allocation request was never submitted, and the reservation was never cancelled either, so the number could not be reused before the next one was allocated. **Two documents were missing and the gap became unrecoverable before anybody discovered it was unnecessary** [[Allocation of Official Aerospace Vehicle MDS Designations][ref_mds_allocation]].

**The X-52 produced nothing of the kind.** The request was made, considered, refused and answered on the same occasion. The programme got a designation. The register records why 52 is absent. **The cost of this refusal, measured in anything the designation system exists to provide, is zero.**

**That is what makes it the cleanest case in the series for asking what the rule is for.** There is no damage to argue about and no cancellation to explain. **There is only a decision, a reason, and an instruction that does not contain the reason.**

## Measuring the Judgement That Was Not Recorded

**The refusal was a similarity judgement, and no measure, threshold or method was recorded with it.** This section states what such a judgement looks like when it is written down, and it is offered as analysis rather than as an account of anything the Air Force did.

**The standard measure of how far apart two strings are is the edit distance**, being the least number of single-character insertions, deletions and substitutions that turns one into the other [[Levenshtein Distance][ref_levenshtein]]. For strings $a$ and $b$ it is computed by the recurrence

$$d_{i,j} \;=\;
\begin{cases}
\max(i, j), & \min(i, j) = 0, \\[4pt]
\min\bigl\{\, d_{i-1,j} + 1, \; d_{i,j-1} + 1, \; d_{i-1,j-1} + \mathbb{1}[\,a_i \neq b_j\,] \,\bigr\}, & \text{otherwise},
\end{cases}$$

with the edit distance being the value at the far corner, where $m$ and $n$ are the lengths of the two strings. **For the pair in question the value is 1**, since substituting B for X is the only operation required. Normalised against the longer string,

$$s(a, b) \;=\; 1 - \frac{d_{m,n}}{\max(m,\, n)} \;=\; 1 - \frac{1}{4} \;=\; 0.75.$$

**Three of the four characters are shared and the three that are shared are the ones the instruction issues in sequence.**

**Spoken, the pair separates slightly further and not much.** Rendered as they would be said, ex fifty two against bee fifty two, the edit distance over words is 1 and over characters is 2. **The part that differs is one syllable at the front and the part that agrees is everything after it.**

**None of this proves the refusal was correct.** A similarity of 0.75 is a number and not a decision, and a decision needs a threshold, and a threshold needs a criterion, and the criterion is the thing that was never written. **What the measurement establishes is that the judgement was of a kind that can be made explicit, and was not.** The literature surveyed below is very largely the record of other people making exactly this kind of judgement explicit, under published criteria, in fields where getting it wrong kills somebody.

### Measuring how alike two strings are

**The largest cluster, and it is the one the refusal actually needed.** Edit distance, phonetic encoding, approximate matching, record linkage and the similarity measures built on them. **AF/A8PE made a similarity judgement in 2006 and recorded no measure, no threshold and no method**, and this shelf is what such a judgement looks like when somebody is required to show their working.

### Names refused because another name was near them

**The same administrative act, performed somewhere it is studied.** Look-alike and sound-alike names, proprietary against nonproprietary naming, and the refusal of a proposed name because an approved one is near it. **A drug regulator does what the Air Force did to X-52A, and does it against published criteria that can be argued with.**

### Call signs, readback and the spoken identifier

**The channel the X-52 would have travelled down.** Call sign confusion, readback and hearback, radiotelephony phraseology and pilot-controller communication. **This is the cluster nearest the reason actually given for the C-16 skip**, which was recorded as concern about confusing an aircraft with the F-16 during the stress of high combat radio traffic.

### Identifiers as administered objects

**Identifiers considered as things somebody has to issue, keep and retire.** Namespaces, numbering schemes, part numbering, registries and unique identifier standards. **A336 surveyed this ground for the X-39 from the direction of absence** and this article approaches it from the direction of collision, which is why the two anomaly surveys overlap here and nowhere else.

### Medication safety, which is where this question is studied hardest

**The discipline that has spent the most money on the consequences of a confusable name.** Medication error, prescribing and dispensing error, pharmacovigilance and the reporting systems built around them. **The pharmacology is not the subject and none of it is cited for any claim here.** What is relevant is that an entire safety literature exists on exactly the judgement the Air Force made without recording one.

### Reading a code, and the shapes of its characters

**The identifier as a thing seen rather than heard.** Legibility, typography, character and digit confusion, and labelling. **The instruction's only written anti-confusion rule for a designator lives in this cluster**, being the prohibition on the series letters I and O because they resemble the digits 1 and 0.

**The smallest cluster in the survey, and that is this section's most useful number.** Aircraft designation, identification and registration as subjects in their own right. **The literature of why the X-52 was refused is almost entirely outside aeronautics**, and an article in a series about aeroplanes should say so rather than pad this row.

## The Source Base

**The twenty-two curated sources that carry the argument and the 1,968 that map the field are different things, and this article keeps them apart on purpose.**

**The evidentiary base is small because the subject is.** Twenty-two curated references and four books carry every claim made here, and the ones that matter most are the three issues of the governing instruction that bracket and follow the decision. **Those are not commentary on the evidence. They are the evidence**, because the central question is what a document does and does not contain, and the only way to settle that is to read it.

**The survey base was harvested and none of it was read.** 4,993 records were retrieved, 2,081 passed the subject gate, and 1,968 reach the reference list after duplicate registrations were removed. **Not one of them is cited in support of any claim about the X-52**, and the article would say exactly the same things if the survey were deleted.

### The Pool

**4,350 records came from the scholarly registry, 621 from the defence report registry and 22 from the space agency's, for 4,993 in total.** 2,081 passed the subject gate and 1,968 survived deduplication into the reference list, across 10 clusters.

### The Report-Primary Fraction Is Near the Bottom of the Series and the Reason Is the Subject

**The report-primary fraction is 1.8 percent, being 36 records of 1,968, which is the second-lowest of the fifty-three articles this series has drafted.** **The lowest is the [X-39][related_post_a336_x39_reserved_never_assigned], the other anomaly written in the reduced order, at 0.0 percent of 2,454 records, and that is not a coincidence.**

**The measure means less here than it does anywhere else in this series and the article will not dress it up.** It counts identifiers issued by the space agency's report server and the defence technical information centre, which is the right instrument when the subject is an aeroplane and the wrong one when the subject is a refusal. **The primary documents this article actually argues from are the three issues of the governing instruction and the registry that records the decision**, and not one of the four carries an identifier the measure can see. **A number that falls because the subject changed is reporting something true**, and the two articles in this series whose subjects are administrative acts rather than aeroplanes are the two at the bottom of this ranking.

### The Literature of This Refusal Is Not an Aeronautical Literature

**Eleven records of 1,968, or 0.56 percent, are about aircraft designation as a subject in its own right.** That is the smallest cluster in the survey and it is the most useful number in this section. **The literature of why the X-52 was refused is almost entirely outside aeronautics**, and an article in a series about aeroplanes should report that rather than pad the row.

### This Is Not a Designation-System Harvest, and the Series Has Already Paid to Learn Why

**A341 ran eight queries for designation systems, nomenclature and classification while writing about the X-44.** They returned 1,510 records, among them Massachusetts tax valuations of 1771, salmonella serotype naming and dental implant designation systems. Thirteen survived an aerospace gate and every one of the thirteen was component nomenclature.

**That measurement is bought and this article did not buy it again.** The harvest was aimed instead at the general question the refusal belongs to, which is the refusal of an identifier because an identifier already in use is near it. **That is the A336 precedent applied to a different anomaly.** A336 surveyed archival silence, classification as infrastructure and the administration of registers, because its subject was a gap. This one surveys confusability and error, because its subject is a collision. **The two overlap in one cluster and nowhere else.**

### Three Sweep-Store Families Had to Be Switched Off, and Every One of Them Was Earned Honestly

**The shared sweep store holds patterns observed to contaminate earlier sweeps, each recorded with the incident that produced it.** Applied to this harvest as it stood before this article added anything to it, and with nothing switched off, it removed 310 records. **Of those, 188 were on subject.**

**Among them was the single most on-subject title in the entire harvest**, being an intervention study on look-alike and sound-alike medication errors for patient safety in healthcare institutions, removed by a pattern reading patient or clinic and annotated medical bleed, seen in most sweeps. **Also removed was the readback and hearback literature**, through the same pattern, because a paper on closing the communication loop said patient safety in its title.

**The store is not wrong. It is aeronautical.** Every medical pattern in it was earned because medicine bleeds into aeroplane sweeps, and it does. **This is the first article in this series whose subject is not an aeroplane**, and refusing a proposed drug name because an approved name is near it is the same administrative act as refusing X-52A because a B-52 exists. **The pharmacology is not the subject. The refusal is.**

**So three tagged families are switched off by name, covering seven patterns between them**, being the medical family, the interpreting family and the teaching family. The mechanism is the tag introduced for A348, which raises on an unknown tag rather than silently leaving a pattern armed, and the article names the tags it uses so that a filter switched off is never indistinguishable from a filter that never existed. **Every other pattern in the store stays armed**, and the store now carries three more that this article earned.

### The Instrument Built to Protect This Survey Deleted Its Subject

**Reading the kept sample found the gate admitting the wrong kind of naming.** Archipelagic sea lanes designation, a specification for grinding wheel nomenclature, and an opinion of the international commission on zoological nomenclature validating a generic name under its plenary powers had all reached the kept set. **A336 met biological nomenclature the same way and recorded it in prose, and A341 met component nomenclature the same way, so this is the third time.** A pattern was written to remove the family for good.

**That pattern removed the article's subject on its first run.** It anchored on the phrase generic name, which is a taxonomic term and is also the pharmacist's term for a nonproprietary drug name. It therefore deleted a paper on the potential hazards of illegible prescriptions with look-alike and sound-alike trade and generic names, and another comparing brand names with generic names. It also anchored on taxonomy, which deleted a controller-to-controller communication taxonomy and a cognitive error taxonomy.

**A filter built to remove naming-that-is-not-this-naming removed this-naming**, and only reading its own drops found it. The pattern now anchors on neotype, lectotype, plenary powers and the commission's case numbering, which are exact.

### The Gate Was Wrong in Both Directions at Once

**The first version was too permissive and too narrow simultaneously**, which is the failure the gate module exists to expose and which only reading both samples finds.

**Too permissive.** A bare human factors anchor admitted an aerial gunner scanner simulator, an extreme cold weather clothing ensemble and a personnel training report, which is the generic-stem failure A370 recorded. A bare character recognition anchor admitted machine-printed Chinese character recognition and handwriting post-processing, which is optical character recognition and has nothing to do with whether a human confuses two glyphs.

**Too narrow.** Phonetic coding methods for Malay name retrieval was refused because the pattern demanded phonetic similarity or phonetic algorithm and the literature wrote phonetic coding. **A paper titled Greek Alphabet and Phonetic Names was refused**, and the phonetic alphabet is the exact mechanism by which a designation is spoken over a radio [[NATO Phonetic Alphabet][ref_icao_alphabet]]. Human Error in Medication was refused because the pattern demanded a noun after the word medication and the title put the noun first.

**Both corrections were made and both samples were read again.**

### A Fabricated Identifier Was Caught Before Assembly

**Four foundational report identifiers were written and one of them did not exist.** It had been written from a plausible title rather than looked up, which is the A347 defect exactly, and it resolved to nothing. **The correct source is better than the invented one**, being the space agency's own study of addressee errors in air traffic control communications, which treats the call sign problem directly [[Addressee errors in ATC communications, the call sign problem][research_callsign_problem]] [[An error-resistant linguistic protocol for air traffic control][research_error_resistant_protocol]] [[Analysis of routine pilot-controller communication][research_routine_pilot_controller]].

**Every foundational identifier is verified against its repository before assembly, and that is why this was found rather than shipped.**

### The Book Identifiers Were Verified on Title and on Author

**All four resolve to the books the labels name** [[Cushing, Fatal words][book_cushing]] [[Reason, Human error][book_reason]] [[Bowker and Star, Sorting things out][book_bowker_star]] [[Norman, The psychology of everyday things][book_norman]]. **The verification was done against a repository search index rather than against the work records**, because the work endpoint returns an internal error for records that plainly exist and returns the same error for keys that do not, so it cannot tell a wrong identifier from a dead one.

## Epistemic State

### Historical Fact

The designation X-52A was requested in 2006 for a programme to test active aeroelastic wing technology, was disapproved because of possible confusion with the B-52 series, and the designation X-53A was allocated to the programme instead. This is recorded by the specialist registry of United States military aerospace vehicle designations [[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]].

The Active Aeroelastic Wing programme began in 1996, first flew in modified form in November 2002, and concluded its flight research in March 2005 [[Active Aeroelastic Wing Flight Research, NASA Facts FS-2005-03-061 DFRC][ref_aaw_factsheet]] [[Flight test of the F/A-18 active aeroelastic wing airplane][research_aaw_flight_test]].

The aircraft was designated X-53 on 16 August 2006 by memorandum of the Air Force Deputy Chief of Staff for Strategic Plans and Programs [[Boeing X-53 Active Aeroelastic Wing][ref_x53_wikipedia]].

The office symbol of the deciding office changed from HQ USAF/XPPE to AF/A8PE with effect from 1 February 2006 [[Allocation of Official Aerospace Vehicle MDS Designations][ref_mds_allocation]].

The F-35 designation was approved on 5 June 2002 against a nomenclature office recommendation of F-24A made on the ground that design numbers are to be assigned consecutively [[Non-Standard DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_nonstandard_mds]].

The requests to redesignate two drones as Q-7 and Q-8, made because the drones were being confused with unmodified aircraft, were refused in March 1954 [[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]].

### Verified by Reading the Document

**The following are statements about the contents of three issues of one instruction and were checked by reading each in full rather than by consulting a description of any of them.**

The 14 April 2005 issue of the joint instruction directs that the coordinating office will assign and reserve the next available consecutive design number within each basic mission for new vehicles [[Air Force Joint Instruction 16-401, 14 April 2005][ref_afi_16_401_2005]].

That issue contains no authority to skip a design number, no occurrence of the word skip, and no criterion by which a proposed design number may be judged unsuitable.

Its duplication check, its trademark search and its four levels of review apply to the popular name and not to the designation.

Its only provision citing confusion in connection with a designator is the prohibition of the letters I and O as series symbols.

The 1994 issue of the same instruction carries the same sequence requirement, the same popular-name duplication check and trademark search, and the same prohibition of I and O, and likewise contains no authority to skip a design number [[Air Force Joint Instruction 16-401, 1994][ref_afi_16_401]].

The 3 November 2020 issue adds, at Attachment 2 paragraph A2.1.6.1.2, that AF/A8PE reserves the authority to skip design number at discretion, and attaches no criterion to it [[Department of the Air Force Instruction 16-401, 3 November 2020][ref_dafi_16_401_2020]].

### Analysis

The edit distance between the strings X-52 and B-52 is 1 and the normalised similarity is 0.75. These are computed and not quoted from any source.

The B-52 entered service in 1955 and is planned to serve into the 2050s, so the numeral has been and will be occupied for something near a century, and had been occupied for fifty-one years at the moment of the refusal [[Boeing B-52 Stratofortress][ref_b52]].

Every collision named in this article is between two designations that differ in the mission symbol and agree in the design number.

The count of cross-series numeric collisions in a system where each mission series counts from one is structurally non-zero and grows as series are added.

### Inference

**That the X-52A refusal was made under an authority the instruction did not then contain is an inference from the absence of any such provision in the 2005 issue**, and an absence in a document is weaker evidence than a presence. It is possible that the authority existed in another issuance not consulted here.

**That the 2020 clause regularised the practice is an inference from its content and its timing**, and no document has been seen that connects the two.

**That the F-35 and X-52 decisions are inconsistent is an interpretation.** The two were made four years apart under one office symbol and then another, and the individuals need not have been the same. **The inconsistency asserted here is of the system and not of any person.**

### What the Record Does Not Settle

**Who asked for X-52A is not stated in any source consulted.** The registry says the request was made for a programme to test active aeroelastic wing technology and does not name the requesting office.

**The date within 2006 is not stated**, so whether the request preceded or followed the 1 February office-symbol change is unknown.

**No document giving the reasoning has been seen.** The phrase possible confusion with the B-52 series is the registry's summary. Whether it quotes the decision or paraphrases it is not determinable from the public record.

**Whether the B-52 was named in the decision because of its fame, its longevity, or its role as the carrier aircraft for X-planes is unknown**, and the third possibility is the one this article finds most interesting and has the least evidence for.

**Whether anyone considered the F-35 precedent in 2006 is unknown.**

**Whether the refusal was correct is not settled by anything here.** No measure and no threshold were recorded, and this article supplies a measure only to show what was missing, not to supply the answer retrospectively.

## Out of Scope

**The Active Aeroelastic Wing itself.** The aeroelastic engineering, the control laws, the roll-reversal problem and the flight-test results belong to the next article in this series, which is about the X-53. **This article names the programme and stops.**

**The unmanned series consuming numbers from the same pool.** The X-58 and X-67 slots were taken by aircraft designated XQ-58A and XQ-67A, which is a different mechanism from a refusal and gets its own articles later in the series [[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]].

**The pre-1962 designation systems.** Everything here concerns the joint system implemented in September 1962 [[1962 United States Tri-Service Aircraft Designation System][ref_tri_service_1962]].

**Foreign designation systems**, and the question of whether they exhibit the same behaviour.

**The surveyed literature itself.** None of it was read and none of it is cited for any claim. It is a map for a reader who wants to pursue the general question and nothing more.

## Conclusion

**The X-52 is the only designation in this series that was asked for properly and refused on the merits of its own string.**

**The instruction in force required the next number in sequence, provided no authority to skip one, and reserved its entire confusability apparatus for the popular name**, which is the half of the identifier it describes as aiding communications and media references rather than the half it calls official. **Its only written anti-confusion rule for a designator is that the series letters I and O are prohibited because they look like digits.**

**The refusal was therefore made under a rule that did not exist in writing, and fourteen years later a sentence was added to the instruction granting the office the authority to skip a design number at discretion, with no criterion attached.** **The issue before the one in force said the same as the one in force, so the authority was absent for at least twenty-six years while the practice ran throughout them.**

**The decision was not eccentric.** It belongs to a long and documented family, and the reasons recorded across that family are operational rather than clerical. A radio under combat load. Maintenance records mixed up between two aircraft built by two companies both called Bell. **In every case the collision crosses a mission boundary, which means that the system's formal guarantee of uniqueness rests on a coordinate that the channel does not carry.**

**And the same office had spent 2002 manufacturing exactly such a collision on purpose**, taking the X-35's number for the F-35 against its own nomenclature office's advice that numbers are assigned consecutively.

**What the X-52 case finally shows is a designation system doing something a numbering scheme is not supposed to do.** A counter does not care what its numbers sound like. **This one does, it has cared since at least 1954, it has never written down what it is listening for, and in 2006 it heard a bomber that had already been flying for fifty-one years and was expected to be flying more than forty years later, and said no.**
